# Verify Findings

Append-only log of adversarial Verify-phase findings. One block per Verify run.

## Run 2026-05-16T13:20

Read substrate: `manuscript/full-draft.md` (897 words; only Section IV present; abstract and thirteen sections marked PENDING). Each persona attacked the present substance (Section IV) and flagged architectural concerns about the unwritten remainder where those affect a present claim. P0 reserved for desk-reject issues; in a one-section partial assembly, the present prose did not surface anything that severe, so all forced findings landed at P1.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section IV.C, final paragraph; Section IV.E, final paragraph — Four bracketed `[TODO: evidence needed for X; see PI-NNN]` markers appear inline in published prose. A JLE peer reviewer reading the stitched draft will treat visible TODO markers as evidence the article is not yet a submission; submission-ready prose cannot contain Markdown build-system placeholders.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 04, rewrite the affected sentences as clean prose hedges (for example, "the input corpus does not include casebook excerpts so far as the author has been able to confirm with the professor") without bracket markers, and move the explicit PI- pointers into a same-section footnote that names the open question without surfacing internal task IDs in body text.
  Routing: Section 04 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section IV.C, footnote [^12] — The footnote cites `https://constitutionallaw.netlify.app` with a "last visited May 16, 2026" parenthetical, then in the same parenthetical concedes "URL liveness verification deferred to next Cite or Verify pass; see PI-005." A "last visited" claim and a "liveness not yet verified" disclaimer cannot coexist in a Bluebook footnote; one of them is wrong.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite pass on Section 04, verify the URL with WebFetch (or substitute an archive.org capture if dead), strip the deferred-liveness parenthetical, and replace the "last visited" date with the actual verification date. If the URL is dead, replace with the archive capture and update PI-005 to closed with the resolution noted.
  Routing: Section 04 cite_status → needs_cite.

- [P2] Section IV.B, paragraph 1 — Filenames are quoted bare (`02_lochner_v_new_york-1.pdf`, `Fulton_v_City_of_Philadelphia_Modernized.pdf`, `merged-incorporation.pdf`, `merged-equal-protection-limitations.pdf`). JLE house style prefers either monospace or a defined typographic convention for filenames; bare inline prose treatment reads as raw-data-dump rather than scholarly description.
  Suggested fix: adopt a consistent treatment for filenames (italic or monospace) and apply it throughout Section IV; document the convention in the article's typographic-conventions footnote in Section I when that section drafts.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section IV.A, paragraph 1 — The corpus is framed as "course-issued materials only: lecture decks, professor-prepared opinion PDFs, merged reading packets, and a small set of assessment artifacts." A reader who teaches Civ Pro from a casebook will read this as the article implying the casebook is irrelevant to the course, when in fact students almost certainly read both the casebook and these PDFs. The article is silent on the casebook's role and that silence cuts against the traditionalist reader.
  Suggested fix: add one sentence to IV.A that acknowledges students also read an assigned casebook, that the input corpus described here is the course-issued layer rather than the full reading load, and that whether casebook excerpts were ingested as input remains an open question (cross-reference PI-001 via the same footnote convention used for the snapshot-date hedge).
  Routing: Section 04 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section IV.D, final sentence — "filename-level organization carries pedagogical intent unevenly, which is the small bridge to Section V's case for a structured note layer rather than further filename cleanup" — a Civ Pro traditionalist will read "structured note layer carries pedagogical intent better" as an implicit claim that the structured layer captures what the live classroom does. The article should not let a filename-hygiene observation slide into a claim about superseding the teaching event itself.
  Suggested fix: rewrite the transition sentence so the bridge to Section V is about machine-readability of an artifact already produced by the professor, not about a structured layer better expressing pedagogical intent than the live class does. A revised sentence might end "which is the small bridge to Section V's case for a structured note layer that machine-readers can traverse, not a claim that any artifact captures the pedagogical event itself."
  Routing: Section 04 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section IV.C, second paragraph — The "casebook excerpts were not ingested" hedge is the right hedge, but it does not tell the reader which downstream claims will change if the answer to PI-001 is "yes, casebook excerpts were ingested." A traditionalist reader is precisely the audience for whom that conditional matters.
  Suggested fix: when Section II and Section XII land, the IV.C hedge should be revisited so it points forward to the specific Section II gap-claim and Section XII privacy-claim that swing on the PI-001 answer. Not actionable this Polish pass; flagged for the next Stitch run to thread cross-references once those sections exist.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section IV.A, paragraph 1 — The architectural claim "The published wiki on Netlify is a static, reviewed website rather than a chatbot or conversational tutor" is doing real load-bearing work for the article's risk argument, and is forward-referenced to Sections III and XII via footnote [^4]. But Sections III and XII are both PENDING. The bare claim cannot yet rest on the article's own internal architecture, and it does not yet cite any chatbot-tutor literature (Kuhail et al., or any of the survey work on educational chatbots in higher ed) to anchor the distinction.
  Suggested fix: add a footnote in IV.A that, in addition to the forward reference to Section XII, cites at least one survey or empirical study of chatbot-tutor architectures in higher education so the IV.A claim is not bare while the downstream sections remain unwritten. Mark for Cite phase to identify the specific source from the Article-Workplan bibliography.
  Routing: Section 04 polish_status → needs_polish (footnote addition; small enough that Polish can take it rather than re-Draft).

- [P1] Section IV.A, paragraph 1, enumeration — The doctrinal scope is enumerated as "judicial review, federalism, separation of powers, the Commerce Clause, executive power, justiciability, preemption, state sovereign immunity, the Reconstruction Amendments, substantive due process, equal protection, the First Amendment, and the Second Amendment." This is the structural-plus-rights subset typical of a one-semester Con Law I course, but the enumeration could read as an implicit claim about what counts as "constitutional law" full stop. Section X's generalization argument will swing on whether the article's method is portable beyond this scope, and the enumeration here pre-frames that question without flagging it.
  Suggested fix: add one sentence after the enumeration that names the scope as the structural-plus-rights subset typical of a one-semester Con Law I sequence, not the full constitutional law curriculum, and pointer-cross-reference to Section X for the generalization argument when Section X drafts.
  Routing: Section 04 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section IV — Three architectural pillars of the AI-in-higher-ed literature do not yet appear anywhere in the present prose: Qian's prompt-engineering framework (relevant to Section VI), Bond et al.'s meta-review (relevant to Sections XI and XIII), and any explicit ethics, equity, accessibility, or digital-divide discussion (relevant to Section XII). Section IV does not need to carry this engagement directly, but the article should not present Section IV as the only window into the input layer without those other sections eventually grounding it.
  Suggested fix: flag for the Harvest phase: when Sections VI, XI, XII, and XIII enter Harvest, prioritize cards that close these literature-engagement gaps. Not actionable on Section 04 itself.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section V.D, third paragraph (the "External literature anchors the architectural claim" block) — Footnote [^29] cites Dong et al., *How to Build an Adaptive AI Tutor for Any Course Using Knowledge Graph-Enhanced Retrieval-Augmented Generation*, to anchor the article's architectural claim about the Obsidian vault. The cited intervention is a chatbot-tutor with KG-RAG retrieval at runtime; the case study is a static, reviewed website with no runtime retrieval. Citing chatbot-tutor literature to support a static-website architectural choice, without an explicit transfer clause, sets up exactly the framing slip that LESSONS L-003 forbids and that Persona 3 will independently flag in education-research terms.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, add one sentence to V.D between the Dong-Peng citations and the closing GraphRAG-cousin line, naming what transfers (the general value of structured retrieval over flat semantic similarity) and what does not (the chatbot-tutor architecture itself); for example, "What transfers from the KG-RAG result is the general value of structured retrieval over flat semantic similarity, not the chatbot-tutor implementation; the vault adopts the structural insight in a static-publication form."
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section V.B, final sentence (the "build script renders the holding bar, citation count, and authority lineage directly off these typed fields" sentence) — The closing phrase "the payoff Section VIII later realizes as a site students browse" presents student browsing as a present fact. The article has not yet established student use anywhere in the present prose; Sections III, XI, and XIII are PENDING and would carry any adoption or learning-outcomes evidence. A JLE peer reviewer treating "site students browse" as an evidenced claim will mark it as an empirical assertion without backing.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, rewrite the closing phrase to remove the implicit-adoption framing, for example "the payoff Section VIII later realizes as a navigable site," or hedge to "a site designed for students to browse." Either form preserves the architectural payoff sentence without claiming a usage fact the article has not earned.
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section V, footnote [^20] and prose throughout V.B — Shortened case-name treatment is inconsistent. V.B prose uses the full *Marbury v. Madison* (1803) on first reference and the bare *Marbury* in a later sentence; the footnote [^20] string is "Marbury v Madison (1803)" inside a vault filename context. Bluebook B10.2 governs short-form usage; the article should adopt a single convention (full name on first reference per section, italicized short-form thereafter) and apply it uniformly within Section V before Stitch tries to enforce it across the whole manuscript.
  Suggested fix: not actionable as a P1 because the prose is intelligible; flag for the next Polish run on Section 05 to standardize short-form usage within the section, and flag for Stitch to enforce the same convention across sections once more drafts land.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section V.C, paragraph 1 (the "Topic page is the synthesis form" paragraph) — The sentence "A single Topic pulls every Case brief and Lecture summary touching a doctrine into one page" uses a passive-voice construction that makes the schema the actor and erases the human curator. A Civ Pro traditionalist reading Section V end to end will note that the professor disappears from V.A through V.D entirely and reappears only at V.E's reactive-iteration story, where the area-enum realignment is described in passive voice as well. The article's thesis depends on professorial judgment being visible in the system, not absorbed into it; Section V as written makes the schema look autonomous.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, add a one-sentence professor-as-curator clause to V.C's opening (for example, "The professor decides which briefs and summaries belong on a Topic page; the schema enforces the structure once that selection is made") and consider mirroring the same actor-naming move at V.E where the realignment decision is described, so the professor is named at least once per subsection rather than only at section boundaries.
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section V.B, second-to-last paragraph (the "Hypothetical Applications block" paragraph) — The sentence "Section VI's prompting design uses it to generate exam-style hypotheticals on a known structural shape rather than free-form text" can be read as a claim that the AI is producing exam content that reaches students. The article has insisted (per L-003 and Section IV.A's static-website-not-chatbot framing) that the deployed site is reviewed; without an explicit reviewed-output framing in V.B, the sentence reads as AI-generated hypotheticals being shipped to learners. A traditionalist will read this as exactly the kind of substitution-of-professorial-judgment claim the article elsewhere disclaims.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, rewrite the sentence to name the review pipeline, for example "Section VI's prompting design uses the block to draft exam-style hypotheticals on a known structural shape; the professor reviews each draft before it lands on a Topic page, consistent with the static, reviewed pipeline described in Section IV.A." This anchors the hypothetical-generation claim inside the human-in-the-loop architecture without adding a new evidence demand.
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section V is silent on the live classroom — no sentence in any of A through E mentions in-class Socratic dialogue, the professor's spoken framing of a doctrine, or the role of live student questions in shaping a Topic page. Section VI presumably carries the prompting-as-pedagogy thread, but a traditionalist reader of Section V alone could conclude that the article positions the vault as the locus of teaching rather than a structured supplement to it. Not actionable on Section 05 prose alone; flag for the Stitch run after Section VI lands to add a cross-reference from V.C or V.E to whichever sentence in VI affirms the live-classroom relationship.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section V.C, paragraph 1 (the "Topic page is the synthesis form" paragraph) — The construction phrase "every Case brief and Lecture summary touching a doctrine into one page" leaves the relevance criterion undefined. AI-in-higher-education methodology treats topic-membership determination as a methodologically loaded choice (manual curation by domain expert, AI-suggested with human review, fully automated by keyword or embedding similarity), and the article will need to be explicit about which method the vault uses if Section X's generalization claim is to land and if any future learning-outcomes claim is to be assessable. Section V.C is currently silent on the methodology, and the source_files frontmatter convention (mentioned later in the same paragraph) implies but does not state manual curation.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, add a clause to V.C's paragraph 1 naming the construction methodology — "Topic-membership is a professor curation choice recorded in the page's source_files frontmatter list rather than an automated similarity computation" — and add a cross-reference forward to Section VI for the prompting design that supports that curation. This closes the methodology gap without adding a new evidence demand.
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section V.D, third paragraph (the "External literature anchors the architectural claim" block) — Independent of Persona 1's framing concern, the Dong et al. effect size (Cohen's *d* = 0.86, *p* < 0.001, n = 76) is presented as supporting the vault's architectural choice without the methodological transfer question being addressed. Dong et al.'s intervention is KG-RAG against pure semantic retrieval inside a chatbot tutor at runtime; the vault performs no runtime retrieval and is not a chatbot. The effect size therefore supports the general claim that structure-aware retrieval beats flat semantic retrieval in chatbot settings; it does not directly support the choice to publish a static website rather than build a chatbot tutor. Citing the statistic to anchor an architectural choice with a different action surface is exactly the misuse-of-empirical-claim move that the AI-in-education literature is sensitive to.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 05, qualify the citation's load-bearing role with the same transfer-clause fix Persona 1 suggested. Both findings can be addressed by a single one-sentence insertion. Optionally, add a parenthetical in footnote [^29] noting that the Dong et al. intervention setting differs from the case-study setting and that the cited effect size supports the value of structured retrieval generally, not the chatbot architecture per se.
  Routing: Section 05 polish_status → needs_polish (the same Polish pass that fixes Persona 1's P1 closes this one).

- [P2] Section V engages two pieces of AI-in-education literature (Dong et al. KG-RAG and Peng et al. GraphRAG survey) but Bond et al.'s meta-review and Kuhail et al.'s chatbot-tutor survey are still absent from any present prose. Section IV.A's prior chatbot-literature P1 (PI-009) remains open against Section 04 and could be addressed by a footnote pointing to the Section V citations once Section V is part of the same manuscript; but the structural absence of Bond et al. (relevant to Sections XI and XIII) and Kuhail (relevant to Section XII) persists. Not actionable on present prose; reinforces the prior P2 flag for the Harvest phase when Sections VI, XI, XII, and XIII enter the rotation.

Read substrate: `manuscript/full-draft.md` (2,824 words; Sections IV, V, and VIII present; eleven sections plus the abstract marked PENDING; Sections VI and VII PENDING between V and VIII so the V-to-VIII transition is structurally non-adjacent per L-030). Section VIII (736 words) is the new prose since the prior Verify tick; Sections IV and V are unchanged and their twelve prior P1 findings (PI-005, PI-006 through PI-010, PI-013 through PI-017) remain open pending the next Polish and Cite ticks on those sections. Each persona attacks the present substance with priority on the new Section VIII prose, follows L-017 (route findings only against sections with drafts), and treats present-section claims that depend on PENDING sections per L-017’s load-bearing rule. P0 reserved for desk-reject issues; the present trio did not surface anything that severe, so all forced findings landed at P1.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section VIII.D, final sentence (the closed-feedback-loop paragraph’s closing line) — “The published site talks back to the vault, and the vault routes the talk-back through the same rotation that produced the page.” The personifying construction (the site “talks back”; “talk-back” as a coined noun) anthropomorphizes the static artifact as a conversational agent, which directly contradicts the static-site-not-chatbot framing the same section asserts at VIII.E and Section IV.A established under L-003. JLE peer review will read this as the article slipping into the exact framing it elsewhere disclaims, and the slip lives in the same section that goes hardest on the distinction.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 08, rewrite the closing sentence to remove the conversational metaphor entirely; for example, “Reader feedback returns to the vault through the page-feedback form, and the next rotation phase processes each submission through the same pipeline that produced the page.” This preserves the closed-loop architectural claim without personifying the static site.
  Routing: Section 08 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section VIII.E, opening sentence — “Section VIII’s most load-bearing claim is the architectural one: the case study is a reviewed static site, not a chatbot.” The self-flagging meta-rhetoric (“Section VIII’s most load-bearing claim is the architectural one”) reads as press-release scaffolding rather than scholarly assertion; JLE authors typically state load-bearing claims directly rather than announcing their structural weight. The construction also creates a small redundancy with VIII.E’s second sentence, which restates the architectural point.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 08, rewrite the opening to state the claim directly without the meta-flag; for example, “The case study is a reviewed static site, not a chatbot.” The colon-introduced second clause becomes the load-bearing assertion in its own right, with no scaffolding required.
  Routing: Section 08 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VIII.B, footnote `[^35]` (now manuscript-global `[^35]`, locally `[^3]` in the polished file) — “`.site/build.py` Python build script, approximately 700 lines” gives an unhedged approximation without a date anchor. JLE convention for vault-internal file-size facts prefers either a precise count tied to an audit date (per the L-011 snapshot discipline) or a hedge like “roughly seven hundred lines” for a date-snapshot consistent with Section IV’s anchor date.
  Suggested fix: not actionable as a P1 because the prose is intelligible and the citation routes through App. A; flag for the next Cite tick on Section 08 to align the line count to a specific audit date matching IV.E’s May 15, 2026 snapshot, or substitute a hedged formulation that does not require date-pinning.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section VIII as a whole — Section VIII describes the deployed website’s architecture (URL, snapshot, build pipeline, deploy phase, feedback loop, static-vs-chatbot posture) in detail but is silent on the site’s pedagogical role. No sentence in any of VIII.A through VIII.E names how this deployed artifact fits into actual Constitutional Law I instruction (whether students consult it during class, after class, alongside the casebook, instead of the casebook, for exam prep, or never), who is the intended audience, or how the professor positions the site within the course. A traditionalist reading Section VIII as the article’s account of the deployed artifact will conclude that the article treats the published site as a freestanding teaching artifact rather than a supplement to live instruction and the assigned casebook, which is the architectural claim Section IV.A’s casebook silence (PI-007) already raised on the input side.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 08, add one sentence to VIII.A (or VIII.D) naming the deployed site as a structured supplement to the casebook and live classroom rather than a substitute for either; for example, “The deployed site is a structured supplement to the assigned casebook and the live classroom, not a substitute for either; Section III describes its intended pedagogical role in the course.” The cross-reference to PENDING Section III matches the convention IV.A’s static-vs-chatbot footnote already uses and does not require Section III to land before the Polish fix can run.
  Routing: Section 08 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section VIII.D, second sentence (the auditable-input/auditable-output pairing) — “the auditable-input commitment pairs with the auditable-output commitment of a reviewed static page.” The “commitment” language uses an unnamed actor as the source of both commitments, which can be read as the system itself committing to auditability; pedagogically, the commitment is made by the professor and the author, not by the published artifact. The actor-erasure pattern mirrors the V.C passive-voice finding (PI-015) and the V.B implicit-adoption finding (PI-014), suggesting a recurring tendency to let the system carry the verbs the people should carry.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 08, rewrite the sentence to name the actor; for example, “The author and the professor commit to auditable inputs and to reviewed outputs; the static-page architecture lets both commitments be inspected.” This preserves the architectural pairing while keeping professorial and authorial judgment visible.
  Routing: Section 08 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VIII is silent on the relationship between the deployed site and the assigned casebook — neither stated nor disclaimed. The casebook silence in Section IV.A (still open as PI-007) compounds across the IV-V-VIII trio without VIII contributing a positioning statement of its own. Not actionable on Section 08 prose alone beyond the Persona 2 P1 above; flag for the Stitch run after Sections II, III, or VI land to weave a consistent casebook-positioning thread through the article rather than asking Section VIII alone to carry it.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section VIII.E, footnote `[^47]` (manuscript-global; locally `[^15]` in the polished file) — Sajja et al. 2023 is cited as the article’s sole chatbot-architecture representative for the load-bearing static-versus-chatbot distinction that Section XII develops. A single platform paper is a thin foundation for a distinction the article makes architectural; the AI-in-higher-education literature includes Kuhail et al.’s 2023 chatbot-tutor survey (named in the workplan’s annotated bibliography) and Bond et al.’s meta-review as the survey-level engagement an AI-in-education reviewer expects. Citing only Sajja et al. leaves the contrast resting on one published platform without acknowledging the field’s mapping of the chatbot-tutor design space.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite tick on Section 08 (per L-024’s rule that Cite owns the load-bearing transfer framing end-to-end when external-literature anchoring is the load-bearing move), add at least one survey-level citation to footnote `[^47]`/locally `[^15]` or to a new VIII.E footnote; the workplan-bibliography Kuhail et al. survey is the obvious candidate, with optional addition of Bond et al.’s meta-review for the broader AI-in-higher-ed mapping. The body prose at VIII.E need not change; the citation density is the missing piece.
  Routing: Section 08 cite_status → needs_cite.

- [P1] Section VIII as a whole — The deployed site is a publicly accessible static website serving structured educational content to an unbounded audience, and Section VIII’s prose says nothing about accessibility (WCAG conformance, alt-text discipline for the holding-bar banner or the four-family grid, screen-reader behavior of the hash-route navigation, keyboard accessibility of the ⌘K search palette), nothing about device or bandwidth assumptions (a JSON-loaded SPA shell is not trivially low-bandwidth), and nothing about the digital divide between students with reliable broadband and those without. PostHog analytics with DNT-respected and session-recording-disabled is mentioned only in footnote `[^37]` (locally `[^5]`); the data-collection practice is silent in body prose. For an AI-in-higher-education reviewer, accessibility silence in a publicly deployed educational artifact is a first-class concern, and Section XII’s risks-and-limits coverage cannot yet bear the load because Section XII is PENDING.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 08, add one sentence to VIII.A (or VIII.D) acknowledging the accessibility, device, and equity considerations and cross-referencing PENDING Section XII for the fuller risks discussion; for example, “Accessibility, device and bandwidth assumptions, and the equity implications of a publicly hosted SPA are first-class concerns the article addresses in Section XII.” The cross-reference pattern matches how IV.A handles the chatbot-architecture forward reference and does not require Section XII to land before the Polish fix can run.
  Routing: Section 08 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VIII.D, prose body — PostHog analytics is named in footnote `[^37]`/locally `[^5]` as “session recording disabled and Do Not Track respected,” but the body prose at VIII.D does not name that analytics are collected at all, what they are used for (debugging, content prioritization, none of these), or what the article does not claim about them (no individual identification, no learning-outcomes inference). The omission is small but matters for a research-ethics reading. Not actionable as a P1 because the footnote does honest minimum work; flag for the next Polish tick on Section 08 to surface the analytics-collection fact briefly in body prose, ideally tied to the Persona 3 P1 accessibility-and-equity sentence so the two additions read as one ethics-aware passage.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section III.C, third paragraph (the Lecture-summary paragraph), final two sentences — “The page captures the third leg of the case-doctrine-classroom triangle Section IV named as the casebook’s blind spot.” Section IV does not use the phrase “case-doctrine-classroom triangle,” does not characterize the lecture summary as a “third leg,” and does not describe any layer as “the casebook’s blind spot.” IV.A enumerates the corpus as four content types (lecture decks, professor-prepared opinion PDFs, merged reading packets, and a small set of assessment artifacts); IV.C discusses what the LMS did not hand (ten missing opinions, the open casebook-input question under PI-001) without any blind-spot framing on the lecture-summary layer. A JLE peer reviewer reading Sections III and IV in sequence will see the cross-reference and check Section IV for the phrase, find nothing, and flag the article for fabricated internal cross-references.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 03, rewrite the III.C cross-reference so it points to what Section IV actually says about the corpus, not what III wishes IV said; for example, “The page captures the lecture-deck content type Section IV.A names as one of the four course-issued artifact families, in a form a student who missed the session can read.” Drop the “case-doctrine-classroom triangle” and “casebook’s blind spot” language entirely, since neither phrase appears anywhere in the article.
  Routing: Section 03 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section III.E, final sentence — “Section III walks the user-experience side first so the reader sees what the case study is before the article argues why the choice was made.” The self-flagging meta-rhetoric (“Section III walks the user-experience side first so the reader sees what the case study is”) reads as press-release scaffolding rather than scholarly assertion, and the construction recurs the same pattern PI-022 flagged at VIII.E’s opening sentence (“Section VIII’s most load-bearing claim is the architectural one”). Two of four polished sections now carry self-announcing meta-rhetoric at a section-boundary sentence; without a Polish-level rule against the pattern it will recur on every subsequent section as it lands.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 03, rewrite the closing sentence to make the structural assertion directly without announcing it; for example, drop the sentence entirely (the preceding sentence already does the forward-pointer work) or replace with “Section XII develops the architectural choice and the risk profile that the user-experience tour does not address.” The article does not need to flag what Section III did; the reader has just finished reading it.
  Routing: Section 03 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section III.D, second paragraph (the five-worked-examples enumeration) — Five vault-generated hypotheticals are described as if they were canonical doctrinal illustrations (“a compelled-retirement-product statute that fails under *NFIB*’s activity-inactivity line; a school-zone firearm statute that fails under *Lopez* and *Morrison*; a *Darby*-with-jurisdictional-overlay statute that succeeds; a *Raich*-style comprehensive-scheme application that succeeds; and a state-internal appliance-recordkeeping statute that is genuinely uncertain”). The article does not disclaim that these are hypotheticals the prompting design produced and the rotation reviewed; a JLE reader could mistake the five examples for the article’s own doctrinal worked examples rather than the vault’s output the article is describing.
  Suggested fix: not actionable as a P1 because III.D’s opening sentence makes the “Hypothetical Applications block” provenance clear; flag for the next Polish tick on Section 03 to add a brief parenthetical on first reference of the five examples (“the five examples on the Commerce Clause Topic page, generated under the Section VI prompting design and reviewed by the professor before publication”) to seal the provenance for any reader who skims into III.D from a section link rather than from III.C.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section III.B, second sentence — “Doctrine-first entry is the affordance the casebook table of contents does not give and the lecture-slide export does not give either.” Many constitutional-law casebooks (Chemerinsky, Stone et al., Brest et al., Sullivan and Feldman, Tribe) organize their tables of contents doctrinally: Federalism, Separation of Powers, Individual Rights, and so on. The claim that the casebook table of contents fails to give doctrine-first entry is empirically wrong for the casebooks a Con Law I student most often uses. A Civ Pro traditionalist reading III.B will read this as the article overclaiming against the casebook tradition the article elsewhere should be respecting; the architectural distinction the article wants to make is not casebook-vs-vault on doctrinal organization but flat-folder-export-vs-typed-schema, and the casebook is on the structured side of that contrast.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 03, rewrite III.B’s second sentence to make the contrast against the Canvas LMS export rather than against the casebook; for example, “Doctrine-first entry is the affordance the Canvas LMS export does not give as shipped, and the lecture-slide export does not give either.” Drop the casebook clause; the casebook is not the contrast class the article is making. Optionally cross-reference Section IV.A’s flat-folder export framing so the reader sees what the contrast actually is.
  Routing: Section 03 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section III.C, third paragraph, closing two sentences — “A student who missed the session can recover the professor’s framing without listening to a recording, and the recovery is structured rather than verbatim.” The “recover” verb and the framing of structured prose as a substitute for the missed live session erase the Socratic dialogue, the in-class hypotheticals, the live student confusion, and the spoken framing that the lecture event actually carries. A traditionalist will read this as the article claiming a Lecture summary supplies what missing the class costs, when in practice missing the class costs the dialogic moments the structured prose cannot capture. The pattern compounds with PI-007 (Section IV.A casebook silence) and PI-023 (Section VIII silence on pedagogical role) by extending the article’s recurring tendency to let structured artifacts carry verbs the live classroom should carry.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 03, rewrite the closing two sentences so the Lecture summary is positioned as a structured supplement to (rather than a recovery of) the live session; for example, “The page captures Professor Chandler’s framing as a structured supplement to the live session; a student who missed the class can read the doctrinal arc and the political-genius framing, though the Socratic dialogue and the live student questions that shaped the session are not recoverable from prose.” This preserves the affordance claim while keeping the live classroom’s irreplaceable work visible.
  Routing: Section 03 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section III as a whole is silent on the casebook’s role in actual Constitutional Law I pedagogy. III.B touches the casebook table-of-contents claim (and overclaims it, see the P1 above), but no sentence in III.A through III.E names whether students read both the deployed site and the casebook, whether the deployed site is positioned as a supplement to the casebook, or how the professor frames the deployed site relative to the assigned reading. The pattern recurs across PI-007 (Section IV.A casebook silence) and PI-023 (Section VIII silence on pedagogical role), making it three of the four present sections silent on the deployed site’s casebook positioning.
  Suggested fix: not actionable on Section III prose alone beyond the Persona 2 P1 fixes above; flag for the Stitch run after Sections II, VI, or VII land to weave a consistent casebook-positioning thread through the article rather than asking Section III alone to carry it. The Section III Polish fix to III.B (replacing the casebook contrast with the LMS-export contrast) and the new III.C structured-supplement framing together carry as much of the load as Section III alone should carry.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section III.E, footnote `[^18]` (Sajja et al. 2023) — Section III.E anchors the load-bearing static-versus-chatbot user-experience contrast on a single platform paper (Sajja et al. 2023), the same paper Section VIII.E already cites at footnote `[^65]` for the same load-bearing distinction at the architectural level. The article now anchors the same architectural distinction in two sections on the same single citation, with the L-034 cross-section verification-reuse convention cleanly applied but the underlying citation-density problem PI-025 already named for Section VIII.E now extending to Section III. The AI-in-higher-education literature includes Kuhail et al.’s 2023 chatbot-tutor survey (named in the workplan’s annotated bibliography) and Bond et al.’s meta-review as the survey-level engagement the field expects when a static-vs-chatbot distinction is the architectural framing of an article; citing only Sajja et al. twice leaves the contrast resting on one platform paper across two sections of the article.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite tick on Section 03 (per L-024’s rule that Cite owns load-bearing transfer framing and citation-density end-to-end when external-literature anchoring is the load-bearing move, mirroring PI-025’s routing for Section 08), add at least one survey-level citation to footnote `[^18]` or to a new III.E footnote; the workplan-bibliography Kuhail et al. survey is the obvious candidate. The body prose at III.E need not change; the citation density is the missing piece, and the same survey citation can serve both Section III and Section VIII once it lands (per L-034 cross-section verification-reuse) so the addition costs one WebSearch retrieval.
  Routing: Section 03 cite_status → needs_cite.

- [P1] Section III as a whole — Section III is the article’s primary student-eye walkthrough of the deployed artifact, narrated as a hypothetical student opening the site and walking through entry shell, Topics index, Topic page, Case brief, Lecture summary, Connections, hypotheticals, and Midpage-verified quotations. Across all five subsections III.A through III.E the prose is silent on accessibility (WCAG conformance, alt-text discipline for the holding bar or four-family grid, screen-reader behavior of the hash-route navigation, keyboard accessibility of the ⌘K palette), silent on device and bandwidth assumptions (a JSON-loaded SPA shell with 198 to 281 pages’ worth of search index loaded client-side is not trivially low-bandwidth), and silent on the digital divide between students with reliable broadband and those without. The hypothetical student the tour follows is implicitly a sighted, mouse-and-keyboard-equipped, broadband-connected reader; the article does not name this assumption or its limits. PI-026 already names this silence as a Section VIII problem; Section III as the article’s flagship student-eye walkthrough compounds the same gap and is the more central place to first acknowledge it.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 03, add one sentence to III.A acknowledging the accessibility, device, and equity considerations and cross-referencing PENDING Section XII for the fuller risks discussion; for example, “The walkthrough that follows assumes a sighted reader on a broadband-connected device with a mouse and keyboard; accessibility, device and bandwidth assumptions, and the equity implications of a publicly hosted SPA are first-class concerns the article addresses in Section XII.” The cross-reference pattern matches IV.A’s static-vs-chatbot forward reference and does not require Section XII to land before the Polish fix can run; pairing the III.A addition with the parallel PI-026 fix at VIII.A would produce a consistent ethics-aware passage at the two sections that most need it.
  Routing: Section 03 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section III walks affordances rather than evidenced learning effects, but the prose does not name the distinction. The hypothetical student “opens,” “lands on,” “follows,” and “clicks” through the site; the article does not say whether real students do these things, in what proportions, with what learning effects, or under what evaluation methodology. Section XI’s evaluation framework presumably carries the methodology, but a research-trained reader of Section III alone could conclude that the article positions affordance description as evidence of educational value, which the AI-in-higher-education literature treats as a methodological category error.
  Suggested fix: not actionable on Section III prose alone beyond the Persona 3 P1 fixes above; flag for the Stitch run after Section XI lands to add a cross-reference from III.A’s walkthrough framing to whichever sentence in XI names the affordance-vs-outcomes distinction explicitly. A one-sentence preface in III.A (paired with the accessibility sentence from the P1 above) could carry both flags in one ethics-and-methodology-aware passage.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section VI.B, paragraph 1, sentences seven and eight — “Section V described the same schema as machine-readable substrate for the generated note. Section VI reframes it as the prompt’s pedagogical commitment, since the templates specify both what an AI-authored note must contain and what the prompt producing it must require.” This is the same self-flagging meta-rhetoric pattern PI-022 flagged at VIII.E’s opening and PI-028 flagged at III.E’s close, now recurring mid-section in VI.B where the prose announces what Section V did and what Section VI is doing rather than letting the reframing stand on its own. A JLE reviewer reading the III-IV-V-VI quartet end-to-end will see the pattern as a habitual structural tic across three sections of the article.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 06, rewrite the two-sentence pair as a single direct assertion of the dual-role claim, for example “The Case Brief schema does both work at once: Section V documents it as the machine-readable substrate the build script parses, and the same fields are the prompt contract Ingest and Enrich fill.” The reframing happens through structure rather than announcement. L-036 should be extended (or a companion lesson noted) so the voice-unity sweep covers mid-section self-pointer constructions in addition to section openings and closes.
  Routing: Section 06 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section VI.D, paragraph 2, final sentence — “The architecture mirrors moot-court and law-review pedagogy, where work meets readers with different reading commitments rather than a single generic reader.” The sentence makes a pedagogical-tradition claim (about how moot-court and law-review pedagogy work) and an analogical claim (that the three-persona Verify architecture “mirrors” that pedagogy) without citation and without hedge. JLE house norms expect pedagogical-tradition claims either to carry a citation or to be hedged as the author’s structural reading rather than asserted as fact.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 06, soften the “mirrors” verb to a structural-analogy claim and either drop the pedagogical-tradition descriptor or hedge it as the author’s reading, for example “The persona architecture is structurally analogous to moot-court and law-review review in placing work in front of readers whose reading commitments differ, though the analogy is to the structural commitment to multiple readings rather than to the live human dynamic those formats turn on.” The hedge resolves both the missing-citation surface and the live-human concern Persona 2 raises against the same sentence.
  Routing: Section 06 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VI.A, paragraph 1, sentence four — “The vault’s prompt library is process-based in Qian’s sense.” The phrase “in Qian’s sense” reads slightly informal for a JLE register. The JLE-idiomatic move would be “in Qian’s typology” or “on Qian’s process-based axis.” Mild copyediting note rather than a load-bearing concern.
  Suggested fix: Polish-time substitution to “in Qian’s typology” aligns the register with the article’s practitioner-scholarly voice and matches the citation-form construction the footnote uses.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section VI.B, paragraph 1, sentences five and six — “The nine-section sequence is the canonical law-school case-brief format. Committing the prompt to that sequence forces every generated brief to do the same analytical work a student is expected to do when briefing a case by hand.” The claim conflates the form of the case-brief template (the nine fixed H2 sections) with the substance of the analytical work a student does when briefing by hand. A student briefing by hand does the cognitive work of issue-spotting, holding-extraction, and doctrinal-fit reasoning; an LLM filling a typed scaffold under a prompt is doing pattern-matching plus generation against the schema, not the analytical work the student would do. The article’s thesis depends on the distinction between what an artifact looks like and what mental work produced it; VI.B as written collapses that distinction.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 06, rewrite the second sentence to name the structural commitment without claiming the analytical-work equivalence, for example “Committing the prompt to that sequence forces every generated brief to follow the same analytical structure a student would use when briefing a case by hand; whether the cognitive labor of analysis transfers to the reader who studies the AI-generated brief is the pedagogical question Section XII develops.” The forward pointer to Section XII handles the open downstream argument without claiming the equivalence in VI.B.
  Routing: Section 06 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section VI.D, paragraph 2 — The Verify prompt with three adversarial personas (Staleness Auditor, Contradiction Hunter, Template Enforcer) plus a focus-mutation table is framed as architecturally parallel to moot-court and law-review pedagogy. A traditionalist will read this as the article confusing the structural form of multiple readings with the pedagogical event of live human readers pushing back unpredictably. Moot court and law-review review are pedagogical because the live humans bring commitments and challenges the author could not have anticipated; templated AI personas keyed off a focus-mutation table produce challenges the prompt designer has already written. The article risks the “confusing being a good editor with being a good teacher” pattern PERSONAS.md flags. The framing also compounds with PI-031’s standing concern in Section III that structured artifacts not be positioned as substitutes for live pedagogical events.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 06, rewrite VI.D’s closing sentence (and the prior “implements adversarial reading at the prompt level rather than as an external review process” sentence) to acknowledge what the persona architecture does and does not replicate, for example “The persona architecture imports moot court’s and law-review review’s structural commitment to multiple reading commitments; it does not replicate the live human readers whose unanticipated pushback is itself part of those events’ pedagogical work.” Pair with the Persona 1 hedge above so VI.D’s final two sentences read as a single calibrated passage on what the architecture is and is not.
  Routing: Section 06 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VI.E, paragraph 2 — “the rule added after *Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump* showed that filling Connections at Ingest seeded broken links.” Footnote [^63]/locally [^13] correctly notes that *Learning Resources* is cited as a vault iteration-history artifact rather than for any judicial holding, which is the right clarification. The body prose, however, could read at first pass as a substantive doctrinal claim about the case. A traditionalist who skips the footnote will momentarily wonder whether the case is being introduced for its holding before the next sentence clarifies otherwise.
  Suggested fix: a small Polish-time clarification in body prose, for example “the rule added after the *Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump* skeleton seeded broken links by filling Connections at Ingest,” would carry the artifact-not-holding framing into the body sentence and let the footnote remain as the documentation.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section VI as a whole — The section’s load-bearing prompt-engineering claims rest on a single survey citation (Qian, footnote [^51]/locally [^1]) anchoring the technique-versus-process typology in VI.A. The typed-schema-as-prompt claim in VI.B is a substantial engagement with the structured-output and JSON-schema-prompting literature (OpenAI structured outputs; the chain-of-density, schema-conditioned generation, and JSON-mode prompting line of work) but cites none of it. The three-persona adversarial-reading architecture in VI.D is a substantial engagement with the multi-agent-debate and self-critique literature (Du et al. 2023, *Improving Factuality and Reasoning in Language Models through Multiagent Debate*; the constitutional-AI line; the self-consistency / self-critique line) but cites none of it. The Verify-prompt-as-pedagogy claim in VI.D would be strengthened by survey-level engagement with the AI-in-higher-education literature (Bond et al.’s 2024 meta-review; Kuhail et al.’s 2023 chatbot-tutor survey, the same workplan-bibliography candidates that resolve PI-025 for Section 08 and PI-032 for Section 03). Section VI is the third section in the article to anchor a load-bearing AI-in-education architectural claim on a single citation; the recurring pattern across PI-025 (Section 08), PI-032 (Section 03), and now Section VI suggests the article needs a planned citation-density pass rather than per-section rework.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite tick on Section 06, add at least one survey-level citation for each of the two load-bearing design moves: one for the typed-schema-as-prompt frame in VI.B (structured-output or JSON-schema prompting survey) and one for the three-persona adversarial reading in VI.D (Du et al. multiagent-debate, or a self-critique survey). Workplan-bibliography Kuhail et al. and Bond et al. resolve the AI-in-higher-ed engagement and can be cross-section-reused under L-034 from any future Cite tick that resolves PI-025 (Section 08) and PI-032 (Section 03) in the same pass; a single multi-section Cite tick can close all three citation-density gaps under the L-028 multi-section pattern. The transfer-clause requirement from L-024 applies to each added citation: name what generalizes from the cited setting (the structural insight) and what does not (the implementation surface) inline in body prose.
  Routing: Section 06 cite_status → needs_cite per L-024 (Cite owns load-bearing transfer framing and citation-density end-to-end when external-literature anchoring is the load-bearing move).

- [P2] Section VI.C, paragraphs three and four — The *exam_readiness* criterion is presented at weight 0.22 of the run score, the *stubs_resolved* criterion at weight 0.22, the *hypos_with_reasoning* criterion at weight 0.14, and so on. Rubric weights are design choices about what the prompt and the run should optimize for; they are not empirical claims about what produces exam readiness, stub resolution, or hypothetical quality in students. The article is careful not to assert learning outcomes from these weights, which is the right move; but a Persona 3 reader will note that the precise decimal weights read at first pass as if they had been tuned against an outcome metric.
  Suggested fix: in a future Polish or Stitch tick (not actionable this round), add one sentence in VI.C or in a footnote noting that the rubric weights are design priors set by the author rather than parameters fit against a measured learning outcome, with a forward reference to Section XI for the evaluation question. The clarification preserves the design-prior framing while distinguishing it from an outcomes-tuned model.

### Persona 1: Skeptical JLE Editor

- [P1] Section VII.A, paragraph 1, sentence 1 — “On March 7, 2026, Andrej Karpathy released `autoresearch`, a roughly six-hundred-thirty-line Python script that delegates the modify, run, evaluate, keep-or-discard loop to a language-model agent and that ran fifty experiments overnight to surface a learning-rate improvement.” The precise line count (“roughly six-hundred-thirty-line”) is an empirical claim about the cited repository that footnote [^1] does not anchor: the footnote enumerates the four design choices, the projected experiments-per-hour figure, and the verified fifty-experiments-overnight figure that L-001 specifically permits, but it does not name the script’s line count. A JLE peer reviewer who follows the citation to verify will find no support for the precision and flag it as exactly the kind of unanchored empirical detail the article’s house-lessons restriction (L-001 on Karpathy figures) is supposed to prevent.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite tick on Section 07, either (a) verify the line count against the repository’s `program.md` and `train.py` files via WebSearch or workspace web_fetch and add a parenthetical to footnote [^1] anchoring the figure, or (b) drop the “roughly six-hundred-thirty-line” qualifier from body prose entirely and let the four-design-choice enumeration in the footnote carry the structural-insight claim. The second option is the cleaner allocation because the line count is not load-bearing for the article’s argument and is exactly the kind of Silicon Valley specificity the article’s practitioner-scholarly voice should avoid.
  Routing: Section 07 cite_status → needs_cite.

- [P1] Section VII.D, paragraph 1, final sentence — “The lessons file is what answers a reader who asks whether the loop is improving or just running.” The sentence makes an implicit empirical claim (the lessons file demonstrates that the loop is improving rather than just running) without naming what evidence would constitute an answer. The artifact (a record of changes) is not the evaluation (whether changes constitute improvement); a reviewer reading this against the article’s recurring sentence at VII.E (“made that judgment reusable, inspectable, and publishable”) will note that the careful hedge at the seam is undercut by the confident assertion at the close of VII.D. The construction also reads as press-release scaffolding (“what answers a reader who asks whether X”) rather than direct scholarly assertion.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 07, rewrite the sentence so it asserts what the lessons file is (a durable record of rule changes the loop has adopted) without asserting that the record demonstrates improvement, for example “The lessons file is the loop’s durable record of rule changes; whether those changes constitute improvement is the evaluation question Section XI takes up.” The forward pointer to Section XI handles the evaluation question without claiming the answer in VII.D. The fix dovetails with Persona 3’s same-sentence P1 below; both Polish-time and Cite-time rewrites converge on the same construction.
  Routing: Section 07 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VII.E, paragraph 1, sentence 5 — “The intake is the architectural feature that distinguishes the vault’s loop from a Karpathy-style autonomous loop.” The definite article (“the architectural feature”) overclaims relative to VII.A and VII.B, which already named two other distinguishing features: VII.A names the professorial gating (“the professor available to gate, defer, redirect, or reverse any phase”) and VII.B names one-phase-per-tick (“the vault commits one phase per tick and lets the rotation itself be the loop”). The sentence reads as a tightening of focus on the page-feedback intake but slips into singular framing where the section has previously been pluralist.
  Suggested fix: in a future Polish tick, soften the article and the noun, for example “The intake is one architectural feature that distinguishes the vault’s loop from a Karpathy-style autonomous loop; the one-phase-per-tick discipline and the professorial gating named in VII.A and VII.B are the others.” Mild copyedit; not load-bearing enough to route to a status flip.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section VII.E, paragraph 1, sentence 1 — “The vault’s answer to professorial control is the page-feedback intake at Step 0 of every scheduled-task run.” The sentence reduces “professorial control” to an asynchronous feedback form. A senior teacher reading this sentence will read “professorial control” and expect the live Socratic exchange where the professor decides in real time what to push on, what to let go, and what to leave for the reader to puzzle out; locating that control in a feedback form filed against a static page is the editor-not-teacher conflation PERSONAS.md explicitly names. The pattern compounds with PI-031 (Section III.C lecture-summary as “recovery” of missed session), PI-039 (Section VI.D personas as moot-court analog), and PI-024 (Section VIII.D actor-erasure on the auditable-input/output commitment) by extending the article’s recurring tendency to position structured artifacts as carrying the verbs the live pedagogical event should carry.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 07, rewrite the sentence so the page-feedback intake is positioned as one channel through which professorial input reaches the loop rather than as the answer to professorial control writ large, for example “The page-feedback intake at Step 0 of every scheduled-task run is one channel through which professorial input reaches the loop; the professor’s live judgment over what the loop produces remains upstream of any artifact the rotation publishes.” The rewrite preserves the architectural payoff of Step 0 while keeping the locus of professorial control where it belongs.
  Routing: Section 07 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section VII.A, paragraph 1, sentence 4 — “Karpathy’s loop runs unsupervised against a measurable scientific objective; the vault’s loop runs against a non-measurable pedagogical objective, with the professor available to gate, defer, redirect, or reverse any phase, and with reviewer comments preferred over the rotation’s own next-in-queue work.” The phrase “non-measurable pedagogical objective” is undefined and inverts the article’s actual practice: every empirical iteration story Section VII tells (the Grier quotation correction in VII.C, the Youngstown joined-vs-concurred clarification in VII.C, the token-problem rubric-split story in VII.D) is an editorial-accuracy success, not a pedagogical one. The vault’s loop measures and improves editorial accuracy and structural consistency; the “pedagogical objective” is what the artifacts are eventually in service of, but the loop does not run against it. A traditionalist reads “non-measurable pedagogical objective” and parses the loop as claiming to improve teaching when the demonstrated improvement is to text.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 07, rewrite the contrast so the vault’s loop is named for what it actually measures (editorial accuracy and structural consistency of the published artifacts) and the pedagogical objective is named as the downstream end the artifacts serve, for example “Karpathy’s loop runs unsupervised against a measurable scientific objective; the vault’s loop runs against measurable editorial-accuracy and structural-consistency criteria on artifacts whose ultimate purpose is pedagogical, with the professor available to gate, defer, redirect, or reverse any phase, and with reviewer comments preferred over the rotation’s own next-in-queue work.” The rewrite keeps the architectural contrast clean while honestly naming what the loop measures.
  Routing: Section 07 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section VII.C, paragraphs 2 and 3 — The Prize Cases / Grier and Youngstown joined-vs-concurred narratives are presented as canonical iteration stories showing the loop catching errors, but the prose does not pause to name what the corrections do for the student-reader: the verbatim Grier language carries weight for an originalist argument about war-powers triggers, and the joined-vs-concurred precision matters for any law student reasoning about precedential weight under Marks. A traditionalist reader who already accepts the loop’s editorial discipline will still want to see the article acknowledge what that discipline buys the student.
  Suggested fix: in a future Polish tick (not actionable this round if Polish picks up the load-bearing P1s first), add one half-sentence at the end of each iteration story naming the pedagogical payoff of the correction; for example, append “The corrected language matters because law students reasoning about war-powers triggers cite the verbatim Grier formulation, not a modernized paraphrase” to the Prize Cases paragraph, and “The corrected attribution matters because precedential weight under Marks depends on which justices joined the opinion and which concurred only in the judgment” to the Youngstown paragraph. Mild rather than load-bearing; flagged for the next Polish tick rather than routed for an immediate status flip.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

- [P1] Section VII as a whole — The section’s load-bearing claims about iterative AI loops, human-in-the-loop architectures, adversarial evaluation, and self-improving systems rest on a single GitHub-repository citation (Karpathy autoresearch, footnote [^1]) plus internal vault references. The AI-systems and AI-in-education literature on human-in-the-loop architectures (Wu et al. on mixed-initiative; the AI-HRI / HCI literature on adjustable autonomy), adversarial evaluation (red-teaming literature; the constitutional-AI line; Du et al. 2023 multiagent-debate already named for PI-040 in Section 06), self-improving systems (continual learning; retrieval-augmented generation surveys; mixture-of-experts), and run-by-run evaluation methodology (HELM; the BIG-bench line; Bond et al. 2024 AI-in-higher-ed meta-review already named for PI-025 / PI-032 / PI-040) is the survey-level engagement an AI-in-education reviewer expects when an article makes architectural claims about iterative AI loops as pedagogy. Section VII is now the fourth section in the article to anchor a load-bearing AI-in-education architectural claim on a single citation (or in this case, on a single GitHub repository), recurring the PI-025 (Section 08), PI-032 (Section 03), and PI-040 (Section 06) pattern in a way that confirms the article needs a planned multi-section citation-density pass rather than per-section rework.
  Suggested fix: in the next Cite tick on Section 07, add at least one survey-level citation each for two of the load-bearing design moves: one for the human-in-the-loop / adjustable-autonomy framing in VII.A and VII.E (a mixed-initiative or human-in-the-loop survey, or the constitutional-AI / RLHF line) and one for the forced-finding adversarial-evaluation architecture in VII.C (Du et al. multiagent-debate or a red-teaming survey, the same Du et al. citation that resolves PI-040 for Section VI). The L-024 transfer-clause requirement applies to each added citation: name what generalizes from the cited setting (the structural insight) and what does not (the implementation surface) inline in body prose. Cross-section reuse under L-034 is available for the Du et al. citation already named in the PI-040 plan, and the Section 07 Cite tick can be folded into the same multi-section Cite pass under L-028 that closes PI-025, PI-032, and PI-040 in one rotation.
  Routing: Section 07 cite_status → needs_cite per L-024 (Cite owns load-bearing transfer framing and citation-density end-to-end when external-literature anchoring is the load-bearing move).

- [P1] Section VII.D, paragraph 1, final sentence — “The lessons file is what answers a reader who asks whether the loop is improving or just running.” The sentence asserts that the lessons file demonstrates iterative improvement without naming what evidence would constitute improvement: a growing list of rules is not the same thing as the system getting better at any measurable task. An AI-in-education reviewer will read this against the loop’s actual evaluation methodology (the per-phase rubric weights footnote [^8] enumerates) and note that the rubric weights are author-set design priors rather than parameters fit against a measured outcome, so the lessons file cannot answer the improvement question on its own. The conflation of artifact (a record of changes) with evaluation (whether changes constitute improvement) is the same form-versus-substance pattern PI-038 flagged at VI.B and the same usability-versus-learning-outcomes pattern Persona 3 flags whenever a learning claim is made without methodology.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish tick on Section 07, rewrite the sentence to acknowledge the gap between artifact and evaluation, for example “The lessons file is a durable record of rule changes the loop has adopted; whether those changes constitute improvement against any measurable criterion is the evaluation question Section XI takes up.” The forward pointer to Section XI handles the question without asserting the answer in VII.D, and the rewrite converges with Persona 1’s same-sentence P1 above so a single Polish-time rewrite resolves both findings. Collapses with Persona 1 P1 above to a single PI tracked once.

- [P2] Section VII.E, paragraph 1 — The page-feedback intake at Step 0 is described as the architectural feature that distinguishes the loop, but the section is silent on who is expected to use the form, what reader feedback the form solicits, and how authorial decisions about what to do with non-professorial feedback are made. The deployed-site URL anchors a public artifact accessible to any visitor; the article does not engage whether student feedback would flow through this channel, how the form’s accessibility (keyboard navigation, screen-reader support, low-bandwidth submission) was designed, or how the loop’s editorial discipline interacts with the equity considerations PI-026 (Section VIII) and PI-033 (Section III) already flagged for the publicly deployed SPA. Section VII is the third section in the article to be silent on accessibility, device, and equity considerations on a publicly deployed feature.
  Suggested fix: in a future Polish tick paired with the PI-026 and PI-033 fixes (so the three sections read as one consistent ethics-aware passage), add one sentence to VII.E acknowledging the accessibility, device, and audience considerations of the page-feedback intake, with a cross-reference to PENDING Section XII for the fuller risks discussion; for example, “The page-feedback intake is open to any visitor of the deployed site, not only the professor; accessibility, device and bandwidth assumptions, and the equity implications of who can submit feedback are first-class concerns the article addresses in Section XII.” Not actionable as a load-bearing P1 on Section VII alone because the three-section accessibility cluster is best handled in a coordinated Polish pass; flagged for the future Polish tick that lands the cluster.

### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section IX.E, paragraph 1, third sentence — “Review maps to the per-tick scorecard reads, the periodic LESSONS Consolidate sweep, and the Verify-phase forced-finding adversarial pass.” The Review row reduces “review” labor to three system-internal mechanisms (a scorecard read by the author, a system-driven Consolidate sweep, a three-persona forced-finding pass) and omits Professor Chandler’s actual review labor — reading drafts, flagging errors, refining prose, approving content for publication — which is the human-in-the-loop role the article’s thesis turns on. The pattern compounds with PI-031 (Section III.C lecture-summary as “recovery” of missed session), PI-039 (Section VI.D personas as moot-court analog), and PI-046 (Section VII.E professorial control as page-feedback intake), making this the fourth section in which the article reduces a live pedagogical-editorial labor to a structured system mechanism; the cost-table is now the surface where the substitution lands quantitatively. A traditionalist reader will note that the article’s recurring sentence promises judgment that is “reusable, inspectable, and publishable,” but the cost-table mapping erases the professorial-judgment hours that produce the reusability in the first place. The L-040 voice-unity rule named the pattern at the prose level; PI-054 extends the same concern to the cost-accounting framing.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 09, rewrite the Review-row mapping to name professorial-review labor as the first component; for example, “Review maps to professorial-review labor — Professor Chandler reading drafts, flagging errors, and approving content for publication — and to the per-tick scorecard reads, the periodic LESSONS Consolidate sweep, and the Verify-phase forced-finding adversarial pass that complement the professorial work rather than substitute for it.” This preserves the four-category mapping while keeping the live-editorial actor visible at the cost-table level.
  Routing: Section 09 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P1] Section IX.E, paragraph 1 (the labor-category-to-phases mapping as a whole) — The four labor categories the workplan names (ingest, prompt tuning, review, debugging) are mapped cleanly onto system phases (vault Ingest plus Article Harvest; per-tick rubric-and-LESSONS work plus Article Outline and Cite; per-tick scorecard reads plus Consolidate plus Verify; per-tick pending-issue resolution plus the multi-tick PI lifecycle), but the four categories themselves exclude the substantive pedagogical labor of a law professor producing a course knowledge system — selecting which cases matter, deciding how to frame doctrinal disputes, drafting class hypotheticals, offering live student feedback. None of the system-phase mapping reaches that work, and the cost-table’s column set as named in the workplan does not have a column for it. A traditionalist reader who teaches Civ Pro for twenty-five years will note that the article’s cost-and-labor accounting captures the labor of operating the maintenance loop while leaving the labor of being a law professor invisible; the recurring sentence is supposed to make that judgment “reusable, inspectable, and publishable,” but a cost-table that does not surface it cannot do the inspecting.

### Persona 3: AI-in-Education Researcher

For an AI-in-education reader trying to assess the resource cost of the method or to compare against their own work, the percentage anchor gives no replicator information without the underlying metric named. The methodological-claim-without-methodology pattern Persona 3’s rubric names exactly.

The fix is one prose insertion in body and one PI-050 closing condition update.

- [P1] Section IX.E, paragraph 1, final sentence — “The table reads as instrumentation of the loop rather than a separate accounting exercise.” The sentence asserts a property of the master cost-and-labor table, but the table itself is deferred under PI-049 (master table reconstruction pending), PI-050 (external pricing lookups deferred), and PI-051 (person-hours backfill deferred); no table exists yet for the reader to read. For an AI-in-higher-ed reviewer who treats cost transparency as central to replicability, asserting that “the table reads as X” when the table is a forward-reference functions as cost-table-as-promise standing in for cost-table-as-deliverable. The article’s most-load-bearing methodologically-transparent move is the cost accounting; making the asserted reading of an absent artifact carry the framing risks the whole methodological-transparency arc.
  Suggested fix: in the next Polish pass on Section 09, rewrite the closing sentence so the framing claim is about the table’s intended construction rather than its present reading; for example, “The table is being assembled as instrumentation of the loop rather than as a separate accounting exercise; the row-level methodology footnote at note [^104] names the construction protocol so a reader can verify the framing once the rows land.” The rewrite preserves the architectural claim without asserting a property of an absent artifact and makes the cost-table-as-promise explicit rather than smuggled.
  Routing: Section 09 polish_status → needs_polish.

- [P2] Section IX engages no AI-in-higher-ed cost-replication literature. Bond et al.’s meta-review and other surveys in the field discuss the cost and labor constraints of AI-augmented course design, the equity implications of subscription-tier requirements, and the institutional resource burdens of running maintenance loops at scale; Section IX’s scope is the article’s own cost record, but the section makes no positioning move against the wider literature on AI-in-education cost-replication concerns. ### Persona 2: Legal Pedagogy Traditionalist

- [P1] Section IV.A, paragraph 1, sentences four and five (the corpus-description and casebook-acknowledgment pair) — “The corpus is course-issued materials only: lecture decks, professor-prepared opinion PDFs, merged reading packets, and a small set of assessment artifacts; students also work from an assigned casebook alongside this course-issued layer, and whether the casebook itself was ingested as input to the vault is an open question this article hedges throughout.” The PI-007 Polish fix added the casebook-acknowledgment clause, which closes the surface gap of casebook-silence, but the corpus description as a whole still erases the professorial-curation moment between the assigned casebook and the course-issued PDFs. Nothing in IV.A names who selected which materials to upload to Canvas, who decided which opinions to merge into reading packets (`merged-incorporation.pdf`, `merged-equal-protection-limitations.pdf`), who chose to issue modernized PDFs for some cases but not others, or why a particular case was course-issued rather than left to the assigned casebook. The corpus is presented as if it arrived as a one-way export from Canvas, while the article’s thesis turns on the proposition that professorial judgment is what the system is in service of; a Civ Pro traditionalist reading IV.A will note that the curation-choice description is exactly the kind of professorial-judgment moment the article needs to make visible at the input-corpus stage, and IV.A as currently polished still leaves it invisible. The pattern recurs the PI-015 (Section V.C passive-voice schema-as-actor erasure), PI-031 (Section III.C lecture-summary recovery substitution), PI-039 (Section VI.D personas-as-moot-court analog), PI-046 (Section VII.E professorial-control-as-form-intake), and PI-054 (Section IX.E review-row erasure) lines, now extending the actor-erasure pattern to the article’s most upstream section.

- [P1] Section IV.E-to-V.A seam — The IV-to-V transition is functional but transactional.
